Desk should be placed deliberately (doctor with back to a wall/window, patient chairs between desk and door side, in view of the door), couch against a wall, shelves/cabinets along a wall.
  - [high] Chair arrangement is odd: the two patient chairs sit side by side on the same long side of the desk and the doctor chair on the short end (d1, d3, d6, d8: doctor at the short end of the desk, patients along the long side), reads as a conference corner rather than a doctor facing a patient. Doctor and patient must sit across from each other (doctor on one long side, 1-2 chairs on the opposite long side), all facing the desk.
  - [medium] Door relation: the desk sits with a chair in the line of sight/entry (d2, d5, d9: desk centred directly in front of the door at 2-3 cells from it, doctor chair against the door-side wall), and in others the whole room between door and desk is empty. Keep the entry aisle clear, a 1 m path to the couch and the sink, and put the desk so the patient walks in towards the chairs.
  - [medium] Exam couch: when present it appears twice (d4: two beds in a 14x10 room, spread on opposite walls) and its open side is not clear/near the cabinet; a doctor's office has one couch (a 5x3 pediatric/GP office at most), with the head against a wall, 1 m free on three sides, a curtain/screen if possible, and a cart/stool beside it.
  - [medium] The cabinet strip (4 small drawers in a row, along a wall or door corner, d6 beside the door) has no function and sits squeezed in corners or next to the door; make it a proper cabinet/shelf wall run or a sink counter with cabinet, not a row of boxes at the door.
  - [medium] Wealth is not visible: squatter, low, high and luxury look identical (same desk, grey carpet). Squatter: scarred table, crates, bucket sink; low: plastic chairs, bare couch; high/luxury: big desk, leather chairs, sofa corner, rug, wall screens, scanner, art, plants.
  - [low] Missing details a real office has: waste bin, scale, plant, wall display/diploma, trolley with instruments, hand-sanitiser and sharps bin by the sink, camera/security for Shadowrun clinics, a second chair for a companion only in bigger rooms.
### consultation_room
- Status: done
- Findings:
  - Blind design (10 sketches, 6x8 to 16x10 cells, street doc to luxury): desk with terminal and doctor chair, 1-2 patient chairs facing the doctor, an exam couch (about 2x4 cells) against a wall with a free access side of 1 m and a privacy curtain, sink with dispenser near the door, med cabinet/shelf, biohazard bin and sharps box, wall screen/anatomy poster; wealth adds plant, scanner/holo display, a seating corner; large rooms get two exam stations. Generated 12 rooms (10x8 to 24x16 cells, squatter to luxury, seeds 1-12) and compared.
  - [high] The room has no medical content: only an office desk, chairs and 1-3 big armchairs (purple blocks). There is no exam couch, sink, med cabinet, sharps/bio bin, scale, diagnostic terminal, curtain or anatomy screen, so it reads as a manager's office or a lounge. A consultation room must have at least an exam couch (long, against a wall, free side) and a sink with soap near the door, always.
  - [high] The armchairs are scattered singly: one against the east wall, one in the SW, one in the NE corner (14x14 luxury: three armchairs spread with a side table each, nothing between them). Seating must be grouped (a facing pair around a small table) or replaced by a patient chair; lone armchairs turned to nobody look random. Max one waiting/relative seat, facing the desk or couch.
  - [high] Large rooms stay empty: 20x14 and 24x16 cells hold one desk and one armchair in a hall of free floor (c7, c9). Rooms of this type should keep a compact size feel; extra area must be filled with a second zone (seating corner, second exam station) or the furniture must scale with area.
  - [medium] The desk is not oriented sensibly: it floats in the middle or by a random wall; the doctor chair is sometimes at the wall with the patient chairs at the door side (good), but in c4 the desk stands against the west wall with chairs left and right and in c8 it sits at the door with the doctor back to the room. The doctor should sit facing the door/room with patient chairs between desk and door, desk close to the back wall and not near the door swing.
  - [medium] Patient chairs are two cubes side by side at one side of a desk, spaced like a table row; one patient chair plus (at most) one relative chair, both facing the doctor, is more realistic; the doctor's chair is half outside the desk footprint in some results (c8).
  - [medium] A side table is attached to every armchair in a random spot (a few cells away) and also stands alone; side tables should touch the seating they serve, otherwise omit them. The small desk-side table in c3 floats at the room centre.
  - [medium] No walkway/clearance logic is visible: in 10x8 and 11x9 rooms the armchair almost touches the desk and the east wall; couch, desk and chair need 1 m between each other and from the door.
  - [low] Missing details by wealth: a plant and wall screen only appear in some seeds; low/squatter rooms should get a cheap cot, a bucket/cooler, luxury should get a holo-display or scanner and art. A terminal appears only at the desk edge in some rooms (j) and not with the desk in others; a desk should always carry a terminal.
  - [low] The generated room alone triggers a single-escape-route warning; normal for a small room, no action.
### fitting_room
- Status: done
- Findings:
  - Blind design (10 sketches, 4x4 m cubicle to 12x8 m gait lab, budget to luxury): one fitting chair/bed in the middle or at a wall with a full-length mirror opposite it (3-way mirror in rich ones), 1.2 m free around the patient, tech stool and a tool/sample cart within reach, wall cabinet or glass case with display limbs, sink, hooks/wardrobe and privacy curtain, terminal desk near the door; bigger rooms add a second fitting station behind a screen, parallel walking bars with a mirror for gait training, a waiting corner, rug and plants for luxury. Generated 10 rooms (10x10 to 24x16 cells, low to luxury, seeds 1-10) and compared.
  - [high] The room scales badly: one single bed in the middle of the floor, whatever the size (24x16 cells = 12x8 m has the same single bed and 80 % empty floor). Larger rooms must add stations (second/third fitting seat with screen), a gait/walking-bars strip, a waiting/dressing corner, more cabinets; small rooms must stay sparse, not cram.
  - [high] The core of a fitting room is missing: no mirror (a full-length mirror wall facing the patient is the defining object), no fitting chair/bench for the technician to work on a limb (only a hospital-style bed with a pillow, which reads as an exam/recovery room), no display of sample prostheses, no privacy screen/curtain, no clothes hooks/wardrobe, no sink. The room reads as a generic treatment room.
  - [high] Everything is scattered randomly along walls and corners with no relationship: shelves/cabinets, the lit double-circle units, the cyan/green small devices and the bench blocks sit at random walls, even in corners next to each other or in the door line (seeds 1, 6, 7, 9: items directly beside the door inside the entry zone; a unit and a cabinet cramped into the south-west corner by the door). Group by function: fitting station (seat, mirror, cart, stool) in one zone, storage/display along one wall, terminal/desk near the door.
  - [high] The bed has no relationship to the rest: no cart or stool beside it except an occasional small round stool, no mirror or equipment facing the foot end, and it points at the door/empty floor. The patient seat must face a mirror or the work wall, with the tool cart and technician stool on the working side and an unobstructed 1.2 m ring around it.
  - [medium] Cabinets and units lean in corners or stand cut off at the room edge (half of a unit hidden in the east wall in seeds 1, 6, 7, 9); several are placed corner-to-corner at the door side. Keep them flush on wall segments with clear space in front (>= 1 m) and not in the door swing.
  - [medium] Duplicate items of one kind are spread apart in different corners (3-4 identical lit units in seed 9 at four places, 2 big wall units on opposite walls) instead of being lined up as a bank along one wall; equipment of one kind should stand together in a row.
  - [medium] Wealth difference is invisible: luxury and low rooms contain the same objects, just more of them; luxury should have rug, sofa/waiting corner, display case, plants and nicer mirror setup, low-end a bare bench and a curtain.
  - [low] Small devices (cyan/green squares) stand free in the open or in corners with no table under them; terminals/scanners should sit on a desk or cart, and the terminal desk should be near the door with its chair.
### recovery_suite
- Status: done
- Findings:
  - Blind design (10 sketches, 8x8 to 20x12 cells, squatter cot room to luxury VIP suite): bed head to the wall with 1 m free on both long sides and foot end; bedside table plus IV stand and vitals monitor at the head; visitor chair beside the bed; wash basin or ensuite bath near the door; wardrobe/cabinet, bin, sofa/armchair group with low table and TV on the wall opposite the bed; 2-4 bed wards in a row with privacy curtains; luxury adds kitchenette, window seat, flowers, art; Shadowrun: autodoc/biomonitor rig, meds cabinet, guard chair, drone dock. Generated 11 rooms (10x10 to 20x12 cells, squatter to luxury, seeds 1-11) and compared.
  - [high] Rooms are far too empty and the contents do not grow with size: the 16x12 luxury suite (r5) and the 20x12 middle suite hold only a bed, an IV stand, a monitor and a corner plant, 80 % of the floor is bare carpet. The squatter, low and middle rooms (r1, r6, r7) are just a bed plus a plant. A recovery room is defined by its nursing kit: add bedside table/locker, visitor chair at the bed, wash basin or small ensuite, wardrobe/med cabinet, waste bin, call button, and for larger rooms a second bed (ward) or a real sitting area.
  - [high] No sink/hand-wash and no bathroom/toilet at all. A post-surgery room always has a hand basin by the door (hygiene) and normally an ensuite WC/shower, at least for middle and above; luxury must have an ensuite and a vanity.
  - [high] The sitting furniture floats: an armchair stands alone in the middle of the floor with a side table next to it (r3, r4, r8, r10), not facing the bed, a TV or a window, with no coffee table. Armchairs/sofas must be grouped (sofa plus armchair around a low table on a rug) against a wall or in a corner, facing the bed or the wall screen; a lone armchair in room centre never happens. In r10 two armchairs sit side by side at the bed's head with a table between, blocking the area where nursing staff reach the patient.
  - [high] Bed placement is unreliable: the bed is pushed into a corner so one long side touches the wall (r5, r8, r10: bed in the corner, no access from that side) or sits at the door-side wall; nursing needs access from both long sides and the foot (at least 1 m) and, for a head-to-wall bed, the head centred on the wall. Keep the bed head on a wall, away from corners, clear of the door swing and the door line.
  - [medium] Monitor, IV stand and bedside table are stuck at the bed head wall in a row (stand, monitor and the green box in the corner). The IV stand and monitor should be right beside the bed head (one on each side or both on the bed's window-side), the bedside table within reach, and the green cabinet/box in the corner should not be hidden behind the bed head.
  - [medium] The wall screen is hung on a side wall facing nothing (r4, r9, r10, r11: on the west wall while the bed is in the east), and in r10 it is split in two places. The TV must face the bed (opposite wall at the foot end) and be visible from the bed and from the sofa.
  - [medium] Sofa lies at the foot of the bed or against the door-side wall (r3, r4) with only a few cells before the bed, so the door-to-bed route is blocked or the sofa back is turned to the bed. Seating belongs on the window/side wall, turned to face the bed or screen; keep a 1 m path from the door to the bed.
  - [medium] Plants appear in two or three corners at every size, including the squatter and low variants (r1, r7, r9), which reads unrealistic for a cheap recovery cot. Limit to one per room and only for middle and above; squatter rooms should instead have a bucket/bin and a bare chair.
  - [low] Suite wealth difference is weak: squatter, low and luxury look almost identical (bed + IV + plant); luxury should visibly add the ensuite, kitchenette/minibar, art, rugs, a second armchair group and a window seat. Shadowrun flavour missing: biomonitor/autodoc rig, cyberware diagnostic console, locked drug cabinet, and a guard chair at the door for shadow clinics.
  - [low] Room is generated with one door only and a warning for one escape route and no rescue window; larger suites (> 14 m2) could have a window/balcony door as second rescue route.
### cyber_surgery
- Status: done
- Findings:
  - Realism goal, priority order: (1) the surgical station around the table must be complete and tight: surgical lights above, anesthesia machine at the head, instrument trolley and back table at the side/foot, monitor, all within arm's reach; (2) the room must scale with its size (a 24x14 room currently looks like an empty hall around one table); (3) wall equipment sorted into zones (scrub/sink by the door, sterile storage, parts/cyberware zone); (4) wealth and style differences; (5) Shadowrun details.
  - Blind design (10 sketches, 10x8 to 24x14 cells, street-doc basement to corp suite): one central table with 1.2 m free on all sides, head against the quiet side; 2 overhead lights (1 gooseneck for the street doc), anesthesia cart at the head, Mayo tray plus back table at the foot/side, vitals monitor, scrub sinks (2-3 in corp) next to the door, sterile cabinets, sharps/waste bins, imaging screen on the wall, cyberware cabinet/parts vault; corp adds a drone surgeon arm, matrix console, observation window and a second door to recovery; gang chop shop has crates and clamp lamps and no sinks; double doors for stretchers. Generated 12 rooms (10x10 to 24x14 cells, squatter to luxury, seeds 1-12) and compared.
  - [high] The room is nearly empty: one table with three small items in the middle and a few units along the walls; at 14x12 and larger more than 60 percent of the floor is bare (r7 20x16 luxury, r9 24x14, r12 20x12). A real operating room is dense around the table and tidy along the walls. The furniture count does not grow with size, so large rooms need either more theatre equipment or a division into zones (prep/scrub area, theatre, parts storage), not just more air.
  - [high] The table station is incomplete and badly arranged: there are no surgical lights above the table (the most iconic OR object), no anesthesia machine at the head, and no back table / second trolley at the foot. The monitor and a single cart sit on the same long side, 1 cell off the table, so the cart blocks one side while the other is bare. Needed: lights over the table, anesthesia + monitor clustered at the head, instrument trolley at the surgeon side, a back table near the foot, and a free ring of about 1.2 m around everything else.
  - [high] The table is tiny and the orientation is always the same (long axis N-S with the head to the north, door south). It also looks like a hospital bed (pillow) rather than an operating table; a surgical table is narrow and has a base, and an arm board / side table on a cyberware surgery (limb work) would be expected. It may be placed as a bed beside the door axis; the door opens directly onto the foot end, which is acceptable, but vary the axis in wide rooms (long axis along the long room axis).
  - [high] No scrub area: sinks are scattered at random (in a corner, next to specimen fridges, or at the far side) in several runs (r3, r11 sinks on the far east wall, r5 and r6 sinks in a far corner). A scrub sink must be at the door (inside the room just beside the entrance or in an alcove), two basins in bigger rooms, with a soap/towel dispenser or waste bin next to it. Also hand disinfectant by the door.
  - [medium] Wall furniture is randomly scattered and the pieces are unrelated: cyberware racks, medical cabinets, specimen fridges and machinery are spread in loose positions with large gaps (r9: one rack on each wall, no pattern). Group them: sterile cabinets and supplies as a continuous run on one wall (counter with cabinets above), the cyberware rack(s) side by side in a dedicated parts zone, fridges together. Do not put units in the entry corners right at the door swing.
  - [medium] Cyberware racks, cabinets and machinery do not read as a surgery: nothing indicates imaging (X-ray / scanner screen), a diagnostic/matrix console, a trays-with-parts cart, an autoclave next to the sink, or a drone surgeon arm. Machinery blocks (autoclave/generator-like objects with round dials) appear in corners next to the door or in the south-west corner of r1/r6 where they are not useful. Add wealth-gated equipment: street-doc quality (one lamp, a cooler, a bucket, no sterile gear), middle (monitor, anesthesia, steriliser), high/luxury (drone arm, imaging, console, observation window, a second door to recovery/prep).
  - [medium] Wealth barely changes the look: low and luxury rooms are the same tabletop with some more cabinets or racks (r1 low vs r5 luxury). Low/squatter should be cluttered, dirty, improvised (crates, mismatched cabinets, bucket, no scrub sink); luxury should have symmetric, tidy equipment, double lamps, glass-fronted parts vault and a viewing window.
  - [medium] Clear walkways are fine (door to table), but the table sits at the exact centre regardless of the room, leaving 5-8 cells empty at the head and far side in narrow rooms while stuff is only along the walls; it should sit in the middle of a free zone with equipment close enough to reach but not blocking the 1.2 m path.
  - [low] Single door, no second exit or door to a prep/recovery space; a large (corp) theatre normally has a clean and a dirty route. Also no waste/sharps bins, no wall clock or wall screen, no ceiling camera, no stool for the surgeon's assistant.
### cyberware_storage
- Status: done
- Findings:
  - Blind design (10 sketches, 3x3 m ripperdoc backroom to 12x8 m depot): shelf units (limb/organ boxes) in parallel rows or along walls with a 1 m aisle facing the shelves; cold storage (fridges/cryo freezers) as a block along one wall; inventory terminal or desk by the door; wall safe or vault; camera; luxury adds glass display cabinets, sterile prep table and a clean mat at the door. Generated 10 rooms (10x8 to 30x20 cells, squatter to luxury, seeds 1-10) and compared.
  - [high] The room is nearly empty and does not grow with size: only racks (5x1 cell strips), 1-3 small fridges and occasionally a safe. A 20x14 or 30x20 cell room has 6-8 objects and 90 % open floor (g4, g6, g10). A storage room is defined by density: item count must scale with area, with shelf rows filling the floor and cold storage as a real block.
  - [high] Racks only ring the walls in scattered 5-cell segments with gaps, corners left empty and no continuous runs; no shelf islands or back-to-back rows ever appear in the room middle, so the room reads as a hall with a few items pushed against the walls. Needs shelf rows in the interior, parallel to each other and perpendicular to the door axis, with 1-1.5 m aisles and a clear central route from the door.
  - [high] Missing working equipment: no inventory terminal/desk, no prep or inspection table, no cart, no label/scanner station, no sink or sterile area. A cyberware store is a managed stock; at least a terminal desk near the door and a prep table are expected, and a corp/luxury room also needs cryo units and display cabinets.
  - [high] Cold storage is too small and scattered: specimen fridges are lone 3-cell units in random wall spots (g1: two fridges on opposite east wall ends; g10: one at the far side, one at the door). Fridges must be grouped as one row/bank on a wall, with more of them in the high-wealth variants, and not split into singletons.
  - [medium] Door area: racks and fridges are tucked right beside the door jamb (g2, g3, g6, g10, g5: fridge/rack against the entrance wall) while nothing like a terminal or guard station sits there. The area directly inside the door should be a clear lobby/standing zone with the access terminal beside it.
  - [medium] Wealth differences are almost absent: squatter, low and luxury rooms use the same racks and fridges; the safe is the only wealth cue and even appears in a low room (g8). Luxury needs glass display cabinets, vault, cryo; squatter needs crates and a single shelf; the tiny/low variants should be cluttered with crates, not clean.
  - [medium] Security details are missing: no camera, no secured vault/cage for premium items, no keypad. The safe (when present) is wedged in a corner (g6, g7, g8) instead of a deliberate position behind the stock.
  - [low] Racks are all 5 cells with the same orientation logic and look identical; mix of 3-6-cell units and a few free-standing crates or boxes would look more lived-in. A rack run occasionally leaves a one-cell gap next to the wall (g1, g2) that reads as an accident.
### body_bank
- Status: done
- Findings:
  - Blind design (10 sketches, 5x4 m chop-shop back room to 16x10 m mega morgue): body drawers or cryo pods in continuous rows against walls (or two facing rows with a wide aisle), 2.5 m clear in front of the drawer faces to pull out trays; organ fridges/dewars in a row on another wall; 1-4 prep tables in a line with 1 m free around; gurney parked near the door; sink/decon and terminal/log desk by the door; door on a short wall, luxury adds glass vault pods in rows, high adds airlock/gown zone and second (dock) door; counts scale with area, the layout stays tidy. Generated 10 rooms (12x10 to 30x20, low to luxury, seeds 1-10) and compared.
  - [high] Cryo pods are scattered singly or in pairs, in corners and at random spots, facing different directions (north, east, south in one room; one pod right beside the door). A body bank's storage must be the dominant, orderly element: pods arranged in one or two straight rows (head ends to the same side, flush to a wall or back to back with a service aisle), never single stray pods or one jammed beside the door.
  - [high] Rooms scale badly: a 30x20 cell (15x10 m) or 24x16 room gets about the same 3-5 pods, a few fridges and a terminal, leaving 80 % of the floor empty. Number of storage units must grow with area (a morgue/body bank is mostly storage); large rooms need many rows of pods plus several fridge/dewar banks, a prep area and a work zone.
  - [high] Specimen fridges are tiny single units sprinkled along walls with gaps (nearly one per wall segment, some in corners). Organ cold storage must be grouped: a continuous bank of fridges/dewars on one wall, evenly spaced, in a row, not scattered in every corner.
  - [high] No work area: no prep/autopsy table, sink or decon, gurney, shelves or records desk. Only pods, fridges and one tiny terminal, so it reads as an empty hall. A real body bank has a transfer/prep table (steel, free around), a sink/decon station, a gurney or trolley parked near the door, and a log desk or terminal workstation (with a chair) near the entrance.
  - [high] No aisles are defined: pods in the north-west corner of the 12x12 room stand tight against each other and the next pod, and pods open towards walls/other pods. The head/hatch side of every pod needs a clear 1.5-2.5 m space for loading a trolley; rows need a common aisle in front.
  - [medium] The terminal is a single 1-cell object hidden in a corner or on an arbitrary wall spot (sometimes beside a fridge in the opposite corner from the door). It must sit beside the door or at the room's front, with a desk and chair, facing the room.
  - [medium] Style/wealth differences are invisible: low, middle, high and luxury all show the same grey pods and fridges. Low should be a few mismatched units with a table and drain; high/luxury: orderly glass vault pods in rows, display terminals, an airlock/decon vestibule, a second door to a loading dock.
  - [medium] Missing detail: no floor drain, no cameras, no warning/biohazard markers, no backup power or coolant unit, no shelves for organ transport boxes, no second exit for staff/goods for the larger rooms.
### docwagon_bay
- Status: done
- Findings:
  - Blind design (10 sketches, 10x8 to 30x20 cells... i.e. 5x4 m squatter garage to 15x10 m luxury hangar): garage door on one short wall (3-5 m wide) plus a personnel door to the clinic on the opposite or side wall; 1 vehicle (2 at 7 m+, 3-4 at luxury) parked nose-out in a straight lane aligned with the garage door, 1.5 m clearance each side and a clear drive lane; along the walls a stretcher/gurney rack, O2 bottles and med-supply cabinet or cage, defib/charging stations, wall gear lockers (trauma-team armour, weapons), bench, tool/parts bench, hose reel and floor drain, eyewash/sink, dispatch screen, extinguisher; wealth adds armoured vans, medevac drone dock, armoury safe, ready-room corner, decon shower; squatter is one old van with barrels and a workbench. Generated 11 rooms (12x10 to 30x20 cells, squatter to luxury, seeds 1-11) and compared.
  - [high] Rooms are far too empty: every result contains one ambulance, 1-2 gurneys, a cabinet or cart and an extinguisher, and nothing else; 70-85 % of the floor is bare (30x20 luxury: one van, three gurneys, a cabinet, and about 500 empty cells). Item count must grow with area and wealth: more vehicles (second van, rapid-response bike, drone) in larger bays, plus the whole wall-side equipment set (lockers, supply shelves, charging stations, tool bench).
  - [high] The vehicle is placed arbitrarily instead of in a parking lane: it floats mid-room or hugs a wall, is often turned lengthwise across the room (seeds 5, 8, 9: standing broadside to the doors), and shows no relationship to the garage door. A bay must park the vehicle with its long axis pointing at the garage door, centred on the door, nose-out or nose-in, with a 3-cell clear lane to the door and 1.5 m clearance beside it; several vehicles stand side by side in a row, not randomly.
  - [high] The vehicle is a plain white rounded slab (about 9x4 cells) with no cab/rear-door/light-bar detail, so it does not read as an ambulance or armed DocWagon van; it sometimes touches the wall (seed 4, 9) so the rear doors cannot open. A rear loading side (stretcher door) must be free with 1.5 m clearance and gurneys parked near it.
  - [high] Doors: two large garage doors at opposite short walls (drive-through) with no personnel door at all; the rest of the building cannot reach the bay on foot, and the two big doors are of different widths (5.5 vs 3.5 cells). A bay needs one wide vehicle door (3+ m) and a normal personnel door to the clinic corridor; drive-through is fine only for big bays and then both doors should be equal vehicle doors.
  - [high] Gurneys are parked in odd places (a gurney in the room's corner, bed across the wall, one standing perpendicular next to the ambulance); they should be lined up in a rack/row against a wall near the personnel door, or beside the ambulance's rear door, and stored head-to-wall, never one random bed.
  - [medium] Missing the defining equipment: stretcher/oxygen/med-supply storage, defibrillator and battery charging station, trauma-team gear lockers and weapons safe (Shadowrun flavour), bench, tool/parts bench, hose reel and floor drain, eyewash/sink, dispatch/status screen, drone dock for higher wealth. Wall-mounted items should run along the walls, the middle stays free for vehicles.
  - [medium] The extinguisher, medical cart and cabinet are dropped in random wall spots and corners (cabinet in the middle of the south wall next to the door, extinguisher in the middle of a long wall, a cart in the door line); equipment should stand in groups on one wall and never narrow the door or the drive lane. Extinguishers belong next to the personnel door.
  - [low] Wealth differences are nearly invisible: squatter, low and luxury bays look the same except count of gurneys. Luxury should show several armoured/medevac vehicles and tidy equipment walls; squatter a single old van with barrels and a workbench.

## common.yaml
### corridor
- Status: done
- Findings:
  - Realism goal, priority order: (1) the corridor must not read as an empty black slab: add the wall-bound fixtures every real corridor carries (exit signs at the ends and at stairs/exits, ceiling/wall lights, cameras by security level, notice board or signage near stairs and lifts); (2) fire equipment (extinguisher, hydrant) in sensible places: next to stairs/lifts/exits and at every ~25 m, always against a wall; (3) type-specific furniture (hotel: ice/vending alcove, trash/linen cart; clinic: benches, sanitizer, wheelchair/gurney parked; office: water cooler, plant, bench at lifts) pressed against walls, never in the middle walking line; (4) dead ends and long stubs fixed, wealth shown.
  - Blind design (10 sketches: 30 m office, 40 m hotel with staggered doors and ice/vending alcove, 20 m clinic with benches/handrail/sanitizer/AED, luxury corp with plants/art/card readers/cameras, 15 m slum with trash, bike and flickering light, short lift lobby stub with bench, L-corridor with corner mirror and fire door at the bend, wide 3 m hall with cooler/vending, corp-lab airlock corridor with cameras every 6 m, 50 m hospital with handrail, parked gurneys, sanitizer, crash cart). Common rules: fixtures on the walls only, keep a clear 1.2 m (more in hospitals), extinguishers at doors/stairs, dead ends short. Generated 12 buildings (office, corp_office, hotel, clinic, hospital, apartment, police, corp_lab, prison; squatter to luxury, 36x24 to 70x50 cells) and compared.
  - [high] Corridors are almost completely empty: across all 12 buildings the only objects in a corridor are a few red extinguisher discs (0-3 per floor) and the odd arrow marker at a door. No exit signs along the route, no lights, cameras, notice boards, benches, plants, vending, carts. Long 60 cell halls (office c1, hotel, hospital) are a featureless dark band; they need a regular rhythm of wall fixtures (a light/sign/camera every ~6 m) and at least one point of interest per segment.
  - [high] Nothing differs by building type or wealth: a luxury corp office, a squatter apartment, a prison and a hotel have the same bare corridor. Expect hotel: ice/vending alcove, cart, room-number plates; clinic/hospital: handrail, benches, sanitizer dispensers, parked wheelchairs/gurneys, wall AED; luxury: plants, art, screens, card readers by doors; slum: trash, crates, broken lights; lab/prison: cameras, card readers, airlock doors.
  - [high] Extinguisher placement is sparse and irregular: single pieces sit in the middle of a long wall, sometimes 40+ m apart, sometimes two stacked together at a stub (c11) and sometimes none on a floor (c3, c4, c5 hotel/clinic corridor). Put one next to each stair/lift door and exit, then every ~25 m, mounted on the wall beside a door, never doubled; a hydrant point and an alarm pull station belong with the stairs.
  - [medium] Corridor width is inconsistent: 3-4 cells (1.5-2 m) in offices, but the hotel and hospital main halls are 6-8 cells (3-4 m) wide, which looks like a hall, not a hotel corridor (c6 hotel: 3 m wide and 35 m long with nothing in it). Residential/hotel corridors should be about 1.5-2 m, clinics 2-2.5 m; if wide, give it seating or alcoves.
  - [medium] Dead ends and stubs without purpose: several side stubs of 4-8 m end in a blank wall (c2 stub down to a toilet, c4 spur at the west, c7/c10 spurs into the room core). A real dead end ends in a door, window, or a feature (bench, plant, vending, fire door), and long dead-ends (building code warnings of 15-25 m) must be avoided in the layout.
  - [medium] Door relations: rooms open directly onto corridors mostly aligned with the opposite door (doors facing each other), which is ok; but in apartment/prison layouts doors open onto narrow strips and onto other rooms rather than the corridor (c8, c11). Doors on opposite sides should be staggered in hotels/clinics (privacy), and no room should have its door swing into the corridor's only walkway if the corridor is under 1.5 m.
  - [medium] Junctions and lift lobbies are bare: at the crossing of two corridors and in front of lifts/stairs there is no widened lobby, signage, bench, or notice board; the corridor in front of the lift (c1, c7) is the same dark band as everywhere. A lift lobby should read as a small waiting node.
  - [low] Corner/bend details missing: no fire-door section at long runs (a door every 30 m splitting the corridor), no corner mirror, no direction signs. Fire doors across a long hall would also create realistic compartments.
### lobby
- Status: done
- Blind design: 10 sketches (corp HQ with curved desk facing the door, turnstile row in front of an elevator bank, seating group and plants; apartment hall with mailbox wall by the door, elevator and stairs, bench, notice board; luxury hotel with 3-station reception, concierge, bell/luggage cart, two sofa groups with coffee tables; budget hotel with small desk, sofa, brochure rack, vending; mid office with desk facing door, visitor chairs, guard desk with monitors, lift bank; Shadowrun megacorp airlock with scanner, guard posts, armoured desk, turrets; slum bare hall with broken mailboxes and a bench; clinic info desk with rows of chairs; mall atrium with kiosk islands; hostel desk with key rack, luggage storage, couch). Common rules: sight line door -> desk -> lifts, desk faces the door, seating as clusters (sofas plus low table) at the walls out of the main path, lift bank on one wall with a clear waiting zone, mailboxes/directory near the door, gates in front of lifts, nothing in front of doors.
- Findings (seen in 11 lobbies: 7 stand-alone 12x10 to 40x24 cells, plus hotel, apartment, hospital, corp office):
  - [high] Big lobbies are mostly bare floor (30x20 and 40x24: ~90 % empty, 3-4 items on the walls). A lobby must be furnished to its size: more seating groups, a bigger or second reception/security station, directory, plants, lift/stair area; or the room must stay small. Empty hall in the middle reads as unfinished.
  - [high] Seating is a single armchair repeated on a regular grid (rows of 3-4 identical chairs spaced evenly in the middle of the floor, each with its own tiny side table). Real lobby seating is grouped: 2 sofas/armchairs facing each other or at right angles around a low table, placed against a wall or in a corner, not floating in a lattice. Singles in the open look like a parking lot; a lone armchair in the centre of the apartment and hospital lobbies is worse.
  - [high] The reception desk has no relation to the door: it sits on the entrance wall beside the door with its back to the room (stand-alone lobbies), or floats in the middle. It must stand opposite or diagonal to the door, facing it, with the clerk chair behind, a clear 2+ m approach zone in front and a path from the door past the desk to the lifts. Often there is no desk at all in non-corp lobbies (no reception, no guard), which is wrong for office/hotel/clinic.
  - [high] No vertical circulation nodes in the lobby itself: no elevator bank, stairs are a small stub in a corner far from the entrance, in the apartment lobby the stairs are in another room. The lobby should lead to the lifts/stairs with a visible waiting area; security gates/turnstiles belong in front of lifts in corp/high-security types.
  - [medium] Wall items are the wrong ones: wall benches/counters appear on random walls and corners (extinguisher, plant, a vending machine/water cooler in a corner, red items near the door) but nothing a lobby needs: mailbox wall (apartment, mixed use), directory/notice board, coat rack, brochure rack, luggage cart (hotel), visitor chairs plus table (office), guard desk with monitors, metal detector/scanner (security), key rack/lockers (hostel). Content should differ per building type and wealth, now office, hotel and apartment lobbies look nearly the same.
  - [medium] Plants and decoration are only tiny corner pots; in rich buildings add big plants, art/logo wall, lounge groups, chandelier-style centre piece. Poor/squatter lobbies should look worn (bare, trash, broken mailboxes, one bench) rather than a smaller copy of the rich layout; wealth currently changes almost nothing visible.
  - [medium] The entrance is a bare gap in the wall: no vestibule/airlock zone, no mat, nothing that funnels entries; items right at the jamb (bin, plant, extinguisher, terminal) narrow the entrance in small rooms (12x10 and 14x14).
  - [low] In a hospital the lobby is a narrow slot between two waiting rooms; the waiting seating belongs in the lobby/waiting area as rows against walls, not separate empty-looking rooms (the waiting rooms show only a few wall items).
### reception_lobby
- Status: done
- Findings:
  - Realism goal, priority order: (1) the desk must scale with the room and look like a desk island/counter (anchored, longer in wide rooms, optionally L-shaped or with a back-office/key-rack wall behind it), not a fixed small block; (2) the waiting area must be a coherent group (seats side by side facing the desk or a low table), not isolated single armchairs on a loose grid; (3) the empty bulk of the room must be filled according to wealth and building type; (4) wall details, circulation and a way onward.
  - Blind design (10 sketches: 8x20 to 36x24 cells, clinic, corp HQ, police, hotel, gang clinic, corp lab, ER, narrow/wide/atrium): desk faces the entrance on the far wall or as an island 4-6 cells in, desk width grows with room (6-12 cells) with chair and terminal behind; seating as a row of 3-4 chairs or a sofa group with coffee table along a side wall facing the desk; plants in corners, brochure rack, coat rack, vending in small rooms; guard post/turnstiles/scanner in police, lab and corp; lifts/stairs and a door to the inner part opposite the entrance; >=2 cells aisle in front of the desk and from door to inner door; poor lobbies are bare with 3 mismatched chairs.
  - Generated 10 lobbies alone (clinic, 10x10 to 30x12 and 12x24, 28x20, squatter to luxury, seeds 1-10) and compared.
  - [high] Reception desk is always the same small 8x3 block floating in the middle of the room, regardless of room size (28x20 and 30x12 rooms have the same desk as 12x10) and not tied to any wall; in big rooms it looks lost, in the 10x10 and 12x10 rooms it takes the room. It should be a counter anchored in a clear position facing the entrance (about a third to half of the room width in larger lobbies), with a back wall/shelves/file cabinets or a second staff seat behind it, and a terminal visible on top.
  - [high] Huge empty floor: in rooms from ~18x14 up well over half the area is bare carpet (the 28x20 lobby has only 4 chairs in a row, nothing in the front half; x7 12x24 has a 10-cell empty stretch between door and desk). A real reception lobby has a seating lounge, a waiting zone with tables, a directory/info stand, plants, a coat rack, etc. Furniture count must scale with area; wealth should add lounges, big plants, art walls, not only more of the same chair.
  - [high] Waiting seats are single armchairs on a grid with a small round side table beside only some of them, all pointing the same way (down/toward the door) rather than toward the desk. Seats should come in groups (sofa or 3-4 chairs in a row/L), arranged around a low coffee table or in a row against the wall, facing the desk or each other; the armchairs must not turn their backs to the receptionist; a side table must belong to each pair, not alternate randomly.
  - [high] No exit onward: the lobby has no inner door/corridor/elevator, only the street door (and a stair stub in some corners). A reception lobby exists to control access to the building, so a second door opposite the entrance (behind or beside the desk), lifts or a security gate must be present, with the desk positioned so the receptionist sees both.
  - [medium] Wall items are the same everywhere: benches on arbitrary wall segments (a bench centred on the top wall far from anything, one on the door wall right beside the door), fire alarm and plants in corners, a red and white item beside the door. Benches on the door wall next to the jamb block the entrance; benches should line up as a row along a side wall facing the room, not scattered one per wall.
  - [medium] The building type and wealth do not show: a luxury 24x16 and a low 18x18 lobby look the same apart from size. Expected differences: security (guard post, scanner, turnstiles) in police/lab/corp, luggage cart and key rack in hotels, kiosk/queue ticket in clinics, bare worn lobbies with 3 mismatched chairs and a vending machine in poor ones, plants, sofas and a logo wall in rich ones.
  - [medium] Walkways are fine but unintended: the open floor is accidental rather than designed. There should be a clear 2+ cell lane from the door to the desk and from the desk to the inner door, with seats pushed out of that line; currently the desk simply sits in the middle and chairs spread evenly.
  - [low] Small items (extinguisher, vending, water cooler, blue glowing item) are placed on the wall at random heights/positions and sometimes in the room's first cell beside the door; keep them to reasonable spots (beside the inner door, in a corner away from the entrance).
### stairwell
- Status: done
- Findings:
  - Blind design (10 sketches: small/large office, hotel, low-wealth apartment, clinic, corp lab, luxury hotel, ground floor, basement, top floor): U-shaped stair of two flights side by side (each >= 1.2 m, wider in clinics), rail or gap between them, a landing at least one flight wide at the door end and a mid landing at the turn, handrails on both sides, a self-closing fire door to the corridor opening in escape direction and clear of the steps, exit sign over the door, extinguisher and alarm panel beside the door, hydrant/riser on the landing, emergency lighting; by type: badge reader and camera (lab), dirt, trash and flicker (low), stretcher-sized landing (clinic), plant/rug/seat (luxury); ground floor = door to the outside, basement = stairs end at a locked door, top = roof hatch. Generated 12 stairwells (office 4 levels incl. basement, hotel 2 stairs on 3 levels, apartment 2 levels, clinic 2 levels) and compared.
  - [high] The stair reads as one flat slab of parallel step lines with a thin centre line and a bare strip at the door end. No up/down arrows, no rails along or between the flights, no turn landing, no handrail. It looks like a ramp or shelf, not a stair. Draw two clearly separate flights with a rail between them and handrails on the outer walls, plus a visible landing at both ends and direction arrows.
  - [high] Every floor looks identical: ground, upper floors, basement and top floor have the same stair. A stair is different by level: ground floor must have an exit door to outside (panic door, exit sign) and a locked/secured door down to the basement; basement bottom has a fire door and dim, dirty look; top floor shows the roof hatch or roof door; upper floors show both up and down. Currently there is no hint of the stair's role.
  - [high] Safety fixtures are inconsistent: some stairwells have an extinguisher/hydrant box beside the door, many (office F1, hotel F1, apartment F1, basement) have nothing visible except the red fire door. A real stair core always has, at the door landing, an extinguisher, an exit sign and an emergency light, and a fire alarm panel/hydrant on the ground floor. These items must sit next to the door and out of the swing, never in the middle of the landing.
  - [medium] Door and landing relation: the fire door is a single door in a corner of the landing end and its swing sweeps over the landing; make sure the landing in front of the steps is at least as deep as the door swing plus 1 m so the door never opens onto the first step. Stair doors should also open towards the escape direction (out of the stair on the ground floor, into the stair on upper floors in many codes), which is not visible now.
  - [medium] The stair is a bare rectangle next to the elevator and often wedged between toilets/nap rooms/storage with no door from those rooms; in the apartment and hotel the stair sits in a corner or between flats and the corridor, which is plausible. But a window or a vent on the landing for outside stairs, or at least a smoke vent on the top, is missing, and stair width (about 4-5 m for 8-12 cells) is wider than a normal 1.2-2 m flight pair in small buildings: small buildings look over-sized, large ones fine.
  - [medium] Building style does not change the stair: low-wealth apartments should show trash, graffiti or a broken lamp; clinics a stretcher-wide landing; corp/high security a badge reader and camera at the door; luxury a plant, bench or decorative landing. All look the same now.
  - [low] Under-stair space is never used (storage nook, bike or cleaning cupboard on the ground floor); a nook door or a few boxes would add life.
### elevator
- Status: done
- Findings:
  - Realism goal, priority order: (1) the car must read as an elevator and have a lobby in front of it: a clear landing of at least 3-4 cells deep and as wide as the car(s) between the doors and the corridor wall opposite, not a door flush onto the corridor edge; (2) give the shaft front its call buttons, floor indicator and door frame details; (3) banks and sizes by building type and wealth; (4) a separate freight/service car and a fire-fighter marking where the building is tall or industrial; (5) lobby dressing and security by wealth.
  - Blind design (10 sketches): small office/apartment one 4x4-cell car with a 4-cell lobby and the stair beside it; mid office two cars side by side with the call panel between and a directory; corp/luxury a bank of 3-4 cars facing each other across an 8-cell lobby with desk, turnstiles, sofa, plant; hotel two guest cars plus a bigger service car, ice/vending alcove and floor plan; hospital two bed lifts (about 5x6 cells, door on the short side) with a 8-cell lobby for turning beds, sanitiser, plus a visitor car; warehouse/factory a 6x8 freight car with gates and a yellow-marked apron; data centre a car with card reader and camera; parking garage an open car by the stair with call button, emergency phone and camera. All doors open into a lobby that is connected to both corridor and stair; same shaft position on every floor.
  - Generated 12 buildings (office, hotel, hospital, clinic, apartment, corp office, warehouse, data centre, parking, 4 floors, low to luxury) and looked at the elevators on several floors; all 11 that have a shaft look the same.
  - [high] No lobby: in every building the elevator door sits directly on the long corridor wall (b1, b3, b5, b9, b10, b11) or is only a 2-cell notch in the wall, with no landing, no widening of the corridor, no seating or sign. The lift just reads as another room with a door. A real lift lobby is a visible clear zone (about 2x the car width, 3-4 cells deep), ideally a small alcove or the corridor junction, in front of the doors.
  - [high] The car is an empty crossed box with no fixtures: no call buttons beside the door, no floor indicator or up/down arrows, no handrail or panel inside, no door leaves drawn as sliding doors in the car. At least the call panel on the wall beside the doors and a hint of car interior (rail, mirror, panel) are needed to make it readable at a glance.
  - [high] Bank layout: two cars are always placed as two identical 4x4 boxes side by side with a thin shared wall (b1, b5, b9, b10, b11). A real bank has the doors flush on one lobby wall with a common lobby and call panel between them; larger buildings (6+ floors, corp, hospital) need 3-4 cars or two opposite groups. Single car in mid-size buildings (b2, b4, b6, b12 data centre) is fine, but a one-car shaft in a hotel/hospital with beds and staff is too few.
  - [high] Hospital and hotel use the same standard car as the office. A hospital needs bed lifts (clearly deeper than wide, about 5x6 cells, doors on the short side) and a clear 8-cell lobby/turning area; a hotel should add a service car near the linen/laundry and keep the guest lift in a nicer lobby. Warehouse/factory should have a large freight car or none at all, not a passenger lift (b10 warehouse shows two small office lifts at the edge of the floor, which is plausible for the office block but there is no goods lift).
  - [medium] Placement is always tucked next to the stairwell with the mechanical room or storage behind it and staff/toilet beside it (b2 elevator wedged between stair and staff room, b6 under a storage room). This is realistic as a core, but the lobby should then be shared with the stair door, as one circulation zone, with the fire door to the stair not opening into the middle of the lift doors.
  - [medium] Parking garage: the elevators are in a corridor core far from the deck instead of beside the parking floor; the deck has no lift lobby, call button, emergency phone or camera. Parking elevators should open onto the deck itself, close to the stairs, with an open lobby.
  - [medium] Apartment buildings (b7, b8) have no elevator at all on 4 floors; four storeys with no lift looks wrong for middle and high wealth (barrier-free access is also required by MBO above the ground floor). Squatter/low may stay stair-only.
  - [medium] Wealth is not visible: luxury corp office and low office show the identical lift. Luxury needs a bank with a bigger lobby, desk or turnstile, sofa and plant; low needs a bare landing, perhaps an out-of-order sign or graffiti; high security needs a card reader and camera at the doors.
  - [low] Fire-fighter lift and signage: no marking, no `no use in fire` sign and no fire extinguisher/alarm button beside the doors; the red alarm button on the corridor wall near the lift is a good start but is not clearly tied to it. A tall building (more than 4 floors) should mark one car as fire-fighter lift with its own lobby.
  - [low] The linen room in the hotel (b3) shows a tiny crossed box (dumbwaiter or goods lift) in the corner without any access; either make it a real service lift with a landing or drop it.
### toilet
- Status: done
- Blind design (summary): 10 variants (small office women/men, unisex single cabin, accessible WC, hotel lobby ladies, cramped bar, club men, mall/station, factory staff, slum). Common model: stalls in one row on a wall with doors facing the room, urinals on a separate wall with dividers and not opposite the door, sinks plus mirror between stalls and exit, a vestibule or offset door so the door gives no view of stalls/urinals, aisle >= 1.5 m, bins at the exit, dispensers at sinks, an accessible cabin with turning space, and count scaling with room size.
- Findings (generated alone in 10x10 to 30x16 and in office/hotel buildings; wealth had no visible effect):
  1. The stalls are never closed: the stall partitions are open at the front and the doors read as free-hanging rather than as a closed cubicle front. A stall row must have a continuous front wall/door line with a gap only at the door, with the pan against the back wall, and a visible partition side wall between neighbours.
  2. Fixture count does not scale with room size: always 3 stalls and 2 sinks, even in a 30x16 room, which becomes a huge empty hall. Larger rooms need more stalls, more sinks, urinals (men) or a second row; small rooms fewer. No empty middle of 10+ m.
  3. Sinks are crammed right beside the entry door on the door wall, with their backs to the wall at the door. They should sit on a wall with a mirror, clearly in the circulation path between stalls and exit, not in the door swing/entry corner.
  4. No privacy: the door opens straight into a view of the whole room and the stall row. A real toilet room has a vestibule, offset door or screen wall at the entrance.
  5. Missing standard items: urinals (men/unisex type), mirrors above sinks, hand dryer or towel dispenser, soap, waste bin (only a single small bin), toilet-paper holders inside stalls, floor drain, one wider accessible stall with grab bars, baby changer in big public rooms, cleaner closet or shaft access.
  6. No gender/usage variant: every toilet looks the same; men (urinals plus stalls), women (more stalls) and a single unisex cabin should differ, and staff vs public.
  7. In the 12x20 and tall rooms the sinks sit in a far corner and the stalls are in the opposite corner with 8 m between them; groups should be adjacent and compact (stalls along one wall, washing area next to them near the door).
  8. Hotel example: stall groups are split into several separate blocks (a row of three plus a separate three plus another) with a narrow dead-end passage between; stalls should form one coherent row, and the sink line along the door wall blocks the corridor-side wall/door swing.
  9. Doors of stalls swing into the aisle area and some overlap neighbouring doors or the walkway; keep swing arcs from colliding and keep a clear 1.5 m aisle in front of the row.
  10. Style/wealth: no difference in dressing (graffiti, broken fixtures in squatter/low; marble/vanity counter and attendant in high).
### stall
- Status: done
- Findings:
  - Blind design (10 sketches): standard office cubicle about 1.0-1.2 x 1.6-1.8 m with the pan on the back wall, paper holder on a side wall, coat hook on the door, door swinging inward and hinged at the back corner; wide accessible stall (about 1.8 x 2.2 m, 0.9 m transfer space on one side, grab rails, pull cord, sink); squat pan with footrests and tap (slum); hotel/luxury stall with bidet and full-height door; clinic stall with sample hatch; low-wealth with broken door/overflowing bin; end stall in a row bigger than the others; Shadowrun flavour (stash behind the cistern, sharps bin, graffiti). Looked at 10 stall rows (toilets in office/hotel/clinic, squatter to luxury) plus 10 stalls generated alone (a bare 10x10 room with only one pan in the corner, so a lone stall does not exist as a unit).
  - [high] Every stall is identical: always a row of exactly 3 equal cubicles of 2x3 cells with a pan and a cistern strip, regardless of wealth, building type or room size. There is no wider accessible stall (about 4x5 cells with space beside the pan, grab rails, wide door opening outward), no end stall that is larger, and no squat or luxury variant. At least one stall per row must be the accessible one.
  - [high] Stalls carry no fixtures besides the pan: no toilet-paper holder on the side wall, no coat hook on the door, no sanitary bin (women), no brush. A cubicle reads as an empty box; each needs at least a paper holder within arm reach beside the pan and a hook/bin by the door.
  - [high] The cubicle shell is not closed: the front partition is only short stubs on both sides of a gap and the door is a small orange line at the left end, so the cubicle looks open towards the room. The front must be a continuous wall/door line with only the door in it, and the door width must be a believable 0.6-0.8 m (now about 0.5 m, narrower than the pan footprint).
  - [medium] Door swing is inconsistent: some doors swing inwards over the pan area (arc inside the stall, only about 0.5 m from the pan front), some swing outwards over the aisle and across the neighbour's door line (rows with the arcs left of the door line). A cubicle needs one convention: door opens inwards with the hinge at the back corner and enough clear floor (about 0.6 m in front of the pan), accessible one opens outwards. Neighbouring door arcs must not touch or overlap.
  - [medium] The pan is a plain oval centred on the back wall and fills the full stall width, with the cistern as a flat grey strip. Pan and seat should be clearly narrower than the cubicle (side clearance of at least 0.2-0.25 m on each side), and the cistern should be a small box behind/over the pan, so the stall does not look like the pan is wedged between the partitions.
  - [medium] Stalls sit in the top-left corner with the partition wall merging with the room wall; no gap above the floor, no partition end posts, no variation of how many stalls per room (always 3), even when the room is 30x16 cells. Counts should follow the room size and the stall block should be flush along one wall with the doors facing the washing area.
  - [low] No wealth/style dressing: squatter/low stalls should have broken or missing doors, graffiti, an overflowing bin, a squat pan; high/luxury stalls should have full-height doors, a bidet and a dimmer light. Shadowrun flavour: a stash behind the cistern, a sharps bin, a vending machine for condoms.
### wc
- Status: done
- Findings:
  - Blind design (10 sketches): office staff WC 1.0x1.5 m to 1.2x2 m, cheap bar WC, shop customer WC with baby table, wheelchair WC 2.2x2.2 m (transfer space, grab rails, turning circle, pull cord), luxury lobby WC, clinic WC with sample hatch, police steel toilet, slum WC with curtain, restaurant WC, exec WC. Common rules: toilet back to the wall opposite or beside the door, 60 cm free in front, basin close to the door and visible from it, mirror, bin, paper holder, soap/dryer, door must not hit fixtures, vent/small window. Generated 12 standalone WCs (12x12 cells, seeds 1-12, squatter to luxury), one 5x6 and WCs in dive_bar and clinic.
  - [high] In a standalone or large WC the toilet and basin are dumped in different corners/walls of a big empty room (12x12 cells = 36 m2 with 2 fixtures, basin 5+ m from the toilet, both far from the door). A single WC is a tiny cell: fixtures must sit together on one or two adjacent walls, a step from the door, with the basin between door and toilet; the room should be sized small, never a hall with two fixtures.
  - [high] Basin placement ignores logic: basin on a wall by itself (even right beside the door or in the opposite corner), sometimes across the room from the toilet. It must be next to the toilet along the same wall or on the side wall, reachable within one step, with free space in front of it.
  - [high] Missing standard details: no mirror above the basin, no hand dryer/towel dispenser or soap dispenser, no toilet-paper holder; only a corner bin appears. Wealth-neutral minimum: mirror, soap/dryer, paper, bin; luxury adds plant/towels, squatter gets a stained basin and graffiti.
  - [medium] Toilet orientation is right (back and cistern to the wall, bowl facing the room) but in several seeds it faces the door straight on from the far wall; prefer the toilet beside the door's line or turned to the side wall so a person on it is not visible through an open door.
  - [medium] Door: the double swing arc (door drawn as double leaf) in a WC is wrong; a single WC has a single 80-90 cm door opening inward without hitting the fixtures or outward into the corridor; the wide opening reads as a hall door.
  - [medium] Windows on every wall (large rooms show 4 windows each side) are unrealistic for an inside WC; a small vent or one frosted window at most, and none when it lies in the building core.
  - [medium] Accessible variant missing: at least one WC per building should be wheelchair accessible (larger, 150 cm turning circle, side transfer space next to the toilet, grab rails, basin with knee space, outward door), as in the clinic where a normal toilet block has no accessible one.
  - [low] In a bar a WC reads fine (toilet in the far corner, basin on the wall), but a bar/dive WC should look worn: graffiti, bin, no mirror glass; clinic WC could carry a sample hatch and a pull cord; Shadowrun flavour: a taped-up stash behind the cistern, a dead-drop panel.

### storage
- Status: done
- Findings:
  - Realism goal, priority order: (1) the room must read as a storeroom: shelving in organised runs and aisles, not a ring of identical strips plus three floating crates in a bare hall; (2) contents must scale with room size so large rooms are not 80 % empty; (3) heavy stock on the floor/pallets and light stock on shelves, with the working end of the room near the door; (4) basic details (fire extinguisher by the door, light, ladder, cart, label desk).
  - Blind design (10 sketches: 3x2 m office supply closet, 2x2 m janitor closet with sink, mop bucket and chemical shelf, 5x4 m shop back stock with 3 rack rows and 1 m aisles, 4x3 m basement tenant cages, hotel linen store with linen cart at the door, 8x6 m warehouse-type store with pallet racking and 3 m forklift aisle, squatter junk store with crate piles and a narrow path, corp archive with lockable cabinets and check-out desk, cold/food store with chest freezer and drain, gang weapon cache with stacked crates, charging bench and camera). Shelves line the walls or stand in rows with 0.8-1 m aisles, crates are stacked in groups against a wall or on pallets, the door side is kept free for a cart/hand truck.
  - Generated 12 stand-alone storage rooms (12x11 to 34x22 cells, squatter to luxury, seeds 1-12, PNG) and compared. Every room looks the same regardless of size or wealth.
  - [high] Rooms above ~16x14 cells are mostly bare floor: a 34x22 room (187 m2) has about 12 shelf strips and 3 crates, the middle 70-80 % is empty carpet. Storage must be dense: freestanding shelf rows (parallel to each other, 1-1.2 m aisles, perpendicular to the door so the aisle leads inward) filling the interior, not only the perimeter. Number of shelves/crates must grow roughly with area; large rooms should be divided into aisles or zones.
  - [high] Storage is only a perimeter ring of identical 4x1 strips with irregular gaps (strips at random spacing, a lone 1-cell fragment, strips stopping short of corners). Shelves should form continuous runs along a wall (back to back, no random gaps) and be the same height class; broken, scattered stubs read as random furniture. Also keep the door side clear: strips beside the door jambs (s5, s10, s12) crowd the entrance.
  - [high] Crates (3 per room, always the same 2x2 brown box) are scattered at random positions in the open floor: some in the middle with nothing around them, some wedged against a shelf or wall corner (s5 against the east wall, s7 against shelf strips). Real stock is stacked in groups on pallets, against a wall or at a loading spot near the door; never single boxes floating mid-room. Vary the size/stack and cluster them, leaving a 1 m path around each group.
  - [high] No relation to the door: nothing sits by the door as a staging/handling zone (hand truck, cart, label table, pallet jack), and there is no clear central route from the door through the room; a crate or shelf can end up straight in the line of the entrance (s3, s7, s9).
  - [medium] No type/wealth variation: a squatter junk room, a luxury archive and a shop stockroom are identical. Expect junk/barrels/tarps and mismatched piles for poor, cabinets/lockable archive cages, a check-out desk and a camera for rich, a utility sink and mop bucket for janitor use, a chest freezer for food stock.
  - [medium] Missing details: no fire extinguisher or exit marker, no ladder/step stool, no trolley, no bin/waste, no label desk or terminal, no camera for valuable stock; light/power points not indicated.
  - [medium] Only one kind of shelf and one kind of box; no floor-standing items (barrels, crates in layers), pallets, steel cages or wall racks, so the room looks flat.
  - [low] The rooms are large with very wide spans and no columns or partitions; a real 20 m wide storage room has pillars or divisions, and a room above ~100 m2 should have a second door.
### utility
- Status: done
- Findings:
  - Realism goal, priority order: (1) it is the wrong kind of room: in all 16 samples (office, hotel, apartment, clinic, hospital, corp_office, supermarket, factory, low/high wealth; always the basement) "utility" is a plant/machinery room, never a janitor/service room: no mop sink, no cleaning cart, no mop/broom rack, no cleaning-chemical shelves, no floor drain; (2) machinery must stand in rows along walls with service clearance, not scattered; (3) rooms are far too big and empty (45-180 cells = 11-45 m2, up to 6x19 m with a 5-cell wide empty void) for a closet; (4) add the missing real fixtures (sink, drain, pipes, electrical panel, ventilation); (5) vary by building type and wealth (all look the same).
  - Blind design (10 sketches): office 4x3 m janitor closet (corner mop sink with rack above, 3-segment shelf on the long wall, cart next to the door, bucket and wringer); hotel housekeeping closet 3x2.5 m (linen shelves on both long walls, cart in the aisle, floor sink at far end, dirty-linen bin); apartment block utility (boiler/water heater in a corner, washer+dryer pair, meter panel by the door, shelf, sink); clinic janitor (mop sink, disinfectant shelf, biohazard bin, paper-towel stock); luxury corp service room (two carts, shelves both sides, floor scrubber on a charging spot, key cabinet at the door); slum block (clutter, rusty sink, buckets, pipes, fuse box, tool bench); electrical room (cabinet row on one wall with 1 m clearance, outward door, extinguisher, no sink); hotel back-of-house (laundry carts, shelf rows with 1 m aisles, ironing board); mall janitor (scrubber, wet-floor signs, staff lockers); corp janitorial plus rack closet. Common rules: 6-20 m2, door on the short wall, wet items on one plumbing wall, shelves along the walls, 1 m aisle, nothing in the door swing, extinguisher by the door.
  - [high] Content does not match the name: a janitor/service room needs a mop (floor) sink with a rack above it, a shelf run for supplies, a cart or bucket parked near the door, and a floor drain; none appear. Machinery rooms (boiler, water heater, ventilation, electrical) are a separate, legitimate room type: either rename this one or give it clearly different content per building type (apartment: water heater, meter, washer/dryer; hotel: linen/housekeeping; clinic/hospital: cleaning plus biohazard bin; office: cleaning closet plus panel).
  - [high] Machines are scattered randomly: pairs of two-fan units stand in the middle of the room, in corners and against walls at random orientations (office-low, hotel-high, corp_office-high). Real plant is lined up along one or two walls in a row, with 1 m service clearance in front, a clear central aisle from the door, and fan/outlet sides facing the wall or the aisle, not the room centre.
  - [high] Rooms are oversized and mostly empty: 6x15 to 9x19 cells with 3-4 small machines leaves large dead floor (corp_office-high, clinic-low, office-low top half is a bare 4x6 void). A real utility/janitor room is 6-20 m2; either shrink it, or fill big rooms with proper plant equipment (tanks, pumps, distribution cabinets, pipe runs on walls) and split off a separate closet.
  - [medium] Door relation: the door often sits at a long-wall end or corner and machines or crates are placed right beside/in front of it (apartment-high crate beside the door, corp_office-low barrel at the door swing, office-high stacked units at the door). Keep the door swing and a 1 m landing clear, the first aisle should lead straight in; the extinguisher and electrical panel go beside the door.
  - [medium] Crates and barrels are dropped singly in the middle of the floor (hotel-low, clinic-high, office-high); real storage is stacked along walls or on a shelf. Barrels stand in groups against a wall or on a drip tray; crates in a stack by a wall, never in the aisle.
  - [medium] Shelving is minimal (0-2 short shelves, in a corner) and the workbench is a lone slab (corp_office-low, hospital-low, hotel-high); a service room has a run of shelving along one wall and a bench only with a tool wall behind it. Shelves must back onto a wall with 1 m of aisle in front.
  - [medium] Missing details a real room would have: electrical distribution cabinet or fuse box on the wall with clearance, pipes/ducts along walls or ceiling, floor drain and sink, ventilation grille, light switch, warning signs, wet-floor signs, first-aid/eyewash, key box. Every sample has exactly one extinguisher, placed anywhere (often mid-wall far from the door).
  - [low] No wealth or condition variation: the same machinery, crates and barrels appear for low and luxury; a wealthy building has neat labelled cabinets and racks, a squatter building has clutter, buckets, tangled pipes. Several machinery objects are tagged squatter wealth even in high-wealth buildings.
### security_room
- Status: done
- Findings:
  - Realism goal, priority order: (1) the room has no working core: a real security office is built around a monitor wall plus a console desk facing it, and here screens, desks and chairs are not grouped; (2) it is far too big and empty for its content; (3) the many small items (cameras/sensors/lockers) are strewn around the perimeter instead of grouped; (4) a guard needs the door and the gear (key cabinet, weapon locker) in a sensible relation.
  - Blind design (10 sketches, 10x8 to 20x14 cells scale from a hut to a megacorp command centre): a monitor wall of 4-12 screens on the wall opposite or beside the door; 1-4 console desks in a row facing it, each with a chair behind and a 1 m aisle behind the chairs; supervisor desk at the back seeing the door; key cabinet, weapon locker/armoury and a row of gear lockers together along one side wall; server/UPS rack, coffee corner (table, fridge, machine), whiteboard/duty roster, first aid, extinguisher by the door; low wealth = one desk, old screens, cot and kettle; high = several consoles, video wall, armoury cage, ready room; hotel/mall add lost-and-found shelf, holding bench. Generated 12 rooms (office, corp_office, hotel, corp_lab, police, casino, data centre, warehouse, hospital, factory; 14x10 to 30x20 cells, squatter to luxury, aaa security) and compared.
  - [high] No monitor wall / console cluster: the room has one or two lone desk-with-screen units (a desk with a chair and a glowing strip on the wall) placed in a random corner or on a random wall (s1, s2, s5, s7, s9), sometimes with the chair on the wrong side or the desk facing a blank wall. There must be a visible control centre: several screens side by side on one wall, console desks in a row directly in front of them, all operators facing the screens, chairs behind desks, a clear aisle behind the chairs. Larger/wealthier buildings get more consoles, not the same single one.
  - [high] Rooms are oversized and mostly bare: 20x14 to 30x20 cells (10x7 to 15x10 m, up to 150 m2) with 10-20 small items hugging the walls and a huge empty middle (s2, s3, s5, s7, s12). A real security room is 15-40 m2 for a building office. Shrink the room or fill the middle with a work area (console rows, a briefing/break table with chairs, a central desk); never leave 70 percent of the floor empty.
  - [high] Small wall items are scattered evenly around the whole perimeter (dozens of cyan 1-cell squares, grey cabinets, strips of 4 lockers, a few red/pink corner markers), with no grouping or relation to each other or to the door. Lockers and cabinets must stand together in one run (key cabinet next to the weapon locker next to the gear lockers), racks and equipment grouped on one side, sensors/cameras only in the corners and above the door, not forming a ring.
  - [medium] Door relation: the lockers run (4 grey cells) and an item sit directly beside the door in the entry wall (s2, s3, s7, s11) and desks sit right at the door wall with their backs to the entrance (s1, s4, s11: desk against the south wall beside the door, chair facing the door-side wall). The guard should sit where the door is visible, the gun locker should not stand in the entry path; the first 1 m inside the door stays clear and the first aisle leads to the desk.
  - [medium] Missing amenities of a staffed 24 h post: no coffee corner/fridge/microwave, no table with chairs for the break or shift hand-over, no cot or sofa, no whiteboard/duty roster/notice board, no first aid kit, no phone/radio charger rack. Wealthier or larger buildings need a break corner, poor ones a simple table, kettle and a chair.
  - [medium] No wealth/building-type variation: squatter (s9), low (s1, s6) and luxury (s7, s12) rooms contain the same objects. Low should be one desk, a mismatched chair, a cot, a kettle, a few old screens; luxury/corp should have a video wall, several consoles, an armoury cage, tidy matching furniture. Hotel/mall type could have a lost-and-found shelf and a holding bench; lab/data centre a biometric door panel and server rack.
  - [medium] Shadowrun flavour missing: no rigger/drone control chair, no jack-in console or deck port, no weapon rack with visible rifles/shotguns behind a cage, no restraint/holding bench, no armour locker; only a key cabinet-like block and screens are recognisable.
  - [low] Single door in the south wall in nearly all samples; real control rooms often have a second door (to the guard room/armoury or a stairwell) and an interior window to the corridor/lobby so the guard can see visitors.
### roof
- Status: done
- Blind design (10 roofs: corp office with helipad, small hotel with terrace, apartment with dishes/clothes lines, clinic, factory with skylight grid, gang squat camp, luxury hotel bar, PV office, data centre with chiller rows, green-roof block): parapet all round, stair/lift head-house at an edge with the door, equipment in aligned rows with 1 m clearance from the parapet and a service aisle, antennas on the head-house, type-specific extras (helipad, PV rows, skylights, terrace group).
- Note: a roof is not part of generated buildings (floors-above N yields N floors, no roof level, also not with entrances roof); it is only reachable via `--room roof`. Reviewed 20 such roofs (office, hotel, apartment, clinic, factory, data centre, corp office, casino, hospital, warehouse; 40x30 and 24x16).
- Findings:
  - [high] Roofs are not part of the buildings at all: no roof level above the top floor, no stair-to-roof connection visible. Multi-floor buildings need a roof floor that matches the stairwell and lift positions.
  - [high] Equipment is scattered at random with no order: HVAC units, round vents and tanks sit at arbitrary spots and angles of placement, sometimes adjacent in odd diagonal clusters. Real rooftop units stand in tidy rows or lines along one side, same orientation, evenly spaced, with a service aisle (about 1 m) in front of the service side.
  - [high] Objects clip into the parapet: HVAC units and tanks are cut by or overlap the outer wall, round vents and ladders half inside the wall. Everything must keep a clear strip (about 1 m) from the parapet; only ladders and the hatch may touch the edge.
  - [high] The roof is almost empty: a 40x30 roof is mostly bare floor with 8-14 items and large dead areas, while the layout has no zones. Needs grouping: a machinery block near the stair/lift head-house, the rest left open or used by a type-specific feature.
  - [high] The same three object kinds (two-fan HVAC, small round vent, white tank) appear for every building type. Missing type character: helipad (corp, hospital, luxury hotel), solar panel rows (office, apartment), skylight rows (factory, warehouse, atrium buildings), dishes and antennas, cooling tower or dry coolers (data centre), terrace with loungers, bar and planters (hotel, casino), junk camp (squatter or low wealth).
  - [medium] Stair/lift head-house is missing: the orange hatch sits as a small box in a corner with no enclosure and no door relation. The stair exit and lift overrun should be a visible block, with a free walkway from it to each machine and to the access door.
  - [medium] The only door is an opening in the south parapet leading nowhere; a roof edge has no door. Entry should be the head-house door; the roof should also have a parapet-height edge without a gap.
  - [medium] Items stand in front of or right next to the access (a vent and a tank near the south opening, a tank beside the hatch in some samples); keep the approach and the zone in front of the stair door clear.
  - [medium] Small round vents are scattered singly and in pairs touching each other and nearby units; they should be grouped by function (a row of exhaust stacks, a cluster beside the kitchen or toilet shaft) or sit on the machinery.
  - [medium] Tanks (white discs) have no supporting detail: no pipes, no platform, no ladder next to them, and they are placed freely in the middle of the roof; water tanks usually stand near the head-house or on a frame at an edge.
  - [low] Details missing: antennas and masts, lightning rod, roof drains and puddles scaled by condition, a window-cleaning rail, safety railing or net, cable runs. Wealth and condition hardly change the look (low wealth should be junkier, luxury tidier).
  - [low] Wealth and size scaling: 24x16 roofs get 4-6 items, 40x30 roofs 12-18, which is roughly proportional but never shows a deliberately empty landing area for drones or helicopters.
### guard_room
- Status: done
- Findings:
  - Realism goal, priority order: (1) group the room into zones (post at the door, lounge/table, locker wall, bunk wall) instead of a lone table in the middle with items scattered along random walls; (2) the room must scale: furniture count must grow with size; (3) lockers/rack as one tidy block, bunks along walls with floor space; (4) door watch: desk/post with a chair facing the door.
  - Blind design (10 sketches: 3x3 m gatehouse, 4x3 m lobby post, 6x5 m prison/police duty room, casino lounge, hotel night guard, 8x5 m corp lounge, slum guardhouse, 8x6 m barracks, 2x3 m booth): desk with chair beside the door facing it, monitor/camera screen on the desk, lockers in one row on a wall, weapon rack/armory cabinet near the door or in a corner, one table with 2-4 chairs, coffee machine/fridge/sink counter, 1-2 bunks or a cot along a wall, 1 m aisles, extinguisher and bin. Generated 10 rooms (office, 20x12 to 60x40 cells, low to luxury, seeds 1-10; object icons unlabeled so identification is by shape: locker row, table with 4 chairs, screen, bunks, rack, toilet-like white items).
  - [high] Empty hall: room sizes up to 60x40 cells (30x20 m) hold the same few objects as a 12x10 room (g3, g5, g8, g10: a single table and 4 chairs in the middle, lockers in one corner, a handful of items on walls; more than 80 percent of the floor is bare). A guard room is a small, dense room; either keep it small (about 4x4 to 6x5 m) or add proportionally more furniture (second table group, more bunks/lockers, counter), never leave a huge empty space.
  - [high] No door post: nothing stands at or faces the door. A guard room's defining feature is the desk/counter/window by the entrance with a chair facing it (and a monitor). Put a desk with chair and screen next to the door (not in the entry line) and orient it towards the entry; the table in the room centre is a mess table, not a watch post.
  - [high] Table and four chairs float exactly in the middle of the room, far from any wall (all 10 results), and is the same in every variant. Place the mess table in a corner/side area near the kitchenette with chairs on 2-3 sides and a clear 1 m aisle, scaled (2 chairs for a small room, 6 for a large one); vary between table, couch+TV, or counter+stools by wealth.
  - [high] Lockers sit in one long row along the top wall starting in the corner (4-12 lockers, g1-g10) and are the same everywhere; 12 lockers (g4, g9) is a locker hall.
